# Design Document: Prompt Pipeline Restructuring

## 1. Overview

This change restructures the two LLM analysis pipelines to create a cleaner separation of concerns. The Session Insights prompt is narrowed to pure knowledge capture (summary, decisions, learnings, session_character), while the PQ prompt is expanded to also produce all session dynamics data (friction_points, effective_patterns, outcome_satisfaction, workflow_pattern, iteration_count, course correction). This consolidation puts all session-behavioral analysis into a single prompt that already sees the full conversation with both user and assistant messages, eliminating the conceptual mismatch of extracting AI-observed dynamics (friction, patterns) from a prompt whose persona is a "staff engineer writing knowledge base entries."

**What is NOT changing:**
- DB schema: `session_facets` table and `insights` table stay exactly as they are, same columns, same types
- Storage locations: facets still go to `session_facets`, insights still go to `insights`
- API route contracts: same endpoints, same request/response shapes
- Normalizers: `friction-normalize.ts`, `pattern-normalize.ts`, `prompt-quality-normalize.ts` are untouched
- Reflect prompts: `reflect-prompts.ts` consumes pre-aggregated data from `shared-aggregation.ts` and is unaffected
- Aggregation queries: `shared-aggregation.ts` reads from `session_facets` and is unaffected

## 2. Current Architecture (Brief)

### Pipeline Flow

```
Session Analysis Pipeline:
  messages ─► formatMessagesForAnalysis()
           ─► SESSION_ANALYSIS_SYSTEM_PROMPT + generateSessionAnalysisPrompt()
           ─► LLM call
           ─► parseAnalysisResponse()
           ─► convertToInsightRows() ──► INSERT into `insights` (summary, decision, learning)
           ─► saveFacetsToDb()        ──► INSERT into `session_facets`
           ─► UPDATE sessions.session_character

PQ Pipeline:
  messages ─► formatMessagesForAnalysis()
           ─► PROMPT_QUALITY_SYSTEM_PROMPT + generatePromptQualityPrompt()
           ─► LLM call
           ─► parsePromptQualityResponse()
           ─► convertPromptQualityToInsightRow() ──► INSERT into `insights` (type=prompt_quality)

Backfill Pipeline (facet-only):
  first/last 20 msgs ─► FACET_ONLY_SYSTEM_PROMPT + generateFacetOnlyPrompt()
                      ─► LLM call
                      ─► extractJsonPayload() + JSON.parse
                      ─► saveFacetsToDb()      ──► INSERT into `session_facets`

Chunked Session Flow:
  analyzeSession() chunks messages, merges insight responses, then calls
  FACET_ONLY_SYSTEM_PROMPT as a follow-up for holistic facets.
```

### What Each Prompt Produces Today

**Session Insights prompt** (lines 259-367 of `prompts.ts`):
- `facets.outcome_satisfaction`, `facets.workflow_pattern`, `facets.had_course_correction`, `facets.course_correction_reason`, `facets.iteration_count`
- `facets.friction_points[]` (with `_reasoning`, `category`, `attribution`, `description`, `severity`, `resolution`)
- `facets.effective_patterns[]` (with `_reasoning`, `category`, `description`, `confidence`, `driver`)
- `session_character`
- `summary` (title, content, outcome, bullets)
- `decisions[]` (max 3)
- `learnings[]` (max 5)

**PQ prompt** (lines 694-811 of `prompts.ts`):
- `efficiency_score`, `message_overhead`, `assessment`
- `takeaways[]` (max 4)
- `findings[]` (max 8)
- `dimension_scores` (5 dimensions)

### Where Data Is Stored

| Output | Table | Column/Type |
|--------|-------|-------------|
| summary, decisions, learnings | `insights` | rows with type=`summary`, `decision`, `learning` |
| PQ analysis | `insights` | row with type=`prompt_quality`, metadata contains scores/findings/takeaways |
| session_character | `sessions` | `session_character` column, direct UPDATE |
| facets | `session_facets` | `outcome_satisfaction`, `workflow_pattern`, `had_course_correction`, `course_correction_reason`, `iteration_count`, `friction_points` (JSON), `effective_patterns` (JSON), `analysis_version` |

## 3. Proposed Architecture

### New Pipeline Flow

```
Session Insights Pipeline (SIMPLIFIED):
  messages ─► formatMessagesForAnalysis()
           ─► SESSION_ANALYSIS_SYSTEM_PROMPT + generateSessionAnalysisPrompt()
           ─► LLM call
           ─► parseAnalysisResponse()
           ─► convertToInsightRows() ──► INSERT into `insights` (summary, decision, learning)
           ─► UPDATE sessions.session_character

PQ Pipeline (EXPANDED — now also writes facets):
  messages ─► formatMessagesForAnalysis()
           ─► PROMPT_QUALITY_SYSTEM_PROMPT + generatePromptQualityPrompt()
           ─► LLM call
           ─► parsePromptQualityResponse()
           ─► convertPromptQualityToInsightRow() ──► INSERT into `insights` (type=prompt_quality)
           ─► saveFacetsToDb()                    ──► INSERT into `session_facets`
           ─► UPDATE sessions.session_character (if returned)

Backfill Pipeline (LEGACY — unchanged):
  Continues to exist for sessions that need facets but don't warrant a full PQ re-analysis.
  Still uses FACET_ONLY_SYSTEM_PROMPT + generateFacetOnlyPrompt().
  Still writes to session_facets only.
```

### What Each Prompt Will Produce

**Session Insights prompt (AFTER):**
- `session_character`
- `summary` (title, content, outcome, bullets)
- `decisions[]` (max 3)
- `learnings[]` (max 5)

**PQ prompt (AFTER):**
- Everything it produces today: `efficiency_score`, `message_overhead`, `assessment`, `takeaways[]`, `findings[]`, `dimension_scores`
- NEW: `session_character`
- NEW: `facets.outcome_satisfaction`, `facets.workflow_pattern`, `facets.had_course_correction`, `facets.course_correction_reason`, `facets.iteration_count`
- NEW: `facets.friction_points[]` (with `_reasoning`, `category`, `attribution`, `description`, `severity`, `resolution`)
- NEW: `facets.effective_patterns[]` (with `_reasoning`, `category`, `description`, `confidence`, `driver`)

### Data Flow: Which Function Saves What, Where

| Function | Writes To | What |
|----------|-----------|------|
| `analyzeSession()` | `insights` table | summary, decision, learning rows |
| `analyzeSession()` | `sessions` table | session_character UPDATE |
| `analyzeSession()` | ~~`session_facets`~~ | **NO LONGER** |
| `analyzePromptQuality()` | `insights` table | prompt_quality row (unchanged) |
| `analyzePromptQuality()` | `session_facets` table | **NEW** — friction, patterns, outcome, workflow, iterations, course correction |
| `analyzePromptQuality()` | `sessions` table | **NEW** — session_character UPDATE |
| `extractFacetsOnly()` | `session_facets` table | unchanged (legacy backfill) |

## 4. Prompt Changes (Detailed)

### 4a. Session Insights Prompt — What to Remove

**Fields removed from output schema:**
- Entire `facets` object: `outcome_satisfaction`, `workflow_pattern`, `had_course_correction`, `course_correction_reason`, `iteration_count`, `friction_points[]`, `effective_patterns[]`

**Guidance constants no longer needed in this prompt:**
- `FRICTION_CLASSIFICATION_GUIDANCE` (~550 tokens) — moves out
- `EFFECTIVE_PATTERN_CLASSIFICATION_GUIDANCE` (~800 tokens) — moves out
- `CANONICAL_FRICTION_CATEGORIES` interpolation in system prompt — moves out
- `CANONICAL_PATTERN_CATEGORIES` interpolation in system prompt — moves out

**System prompt changes:**

Remove from `SESSION_ANALYSIS_SYSTEM_PROMPT` (lines 259-367):
- All of `PART 1 — SESSION FACETS` (lines 263-299): the entire facet extraction section including outcome_satisfaction, workflow_pattern, friction_points specification, effective_patterns specification, had_course_correction, course_correction_reason, iteration_count, and the two inline guidance constants
- The sentence "If the session has minimal friction and straightforward execution..." (line 299)

Rename what remains:
- `PART 2 — INSIGHTS` becomes the main (and only) body. Remove the "PART 2" heading and "(then extract these)" qualification.

The system prompt persona stays the same: "You are a senior staff engineer writing entries for a team's engineering knowledge base."

**New simplified system prompt** (conceptual — exact wording for the developer):

```
You are a senior staff engineer writing entries for a team's engineering knowledge base. You've just observed an AI-assisted coding session and your job is to extract the insights that would save another engineer time if they encountered a similar situation 6 months from now.

Your audience is a developer who has never seen this session but works on the same codebase. They need enough context to understand WHY a decision was made, WHAT specific gotcha was discovered, and WHEN this knowledge applies.

You will extract:
1. **Summary**: A narrative of what was accomplished and the outcome
2. **Decisions**: Technical choices made — with full situation context, reasoning, rejected alternatives, trade-offs, and conditions for revisiting (max 3)
3. **Learnings**: Technical discoveries, gotchas, debugging breakthroughs — with the observable symptom, root cause, and a transferable takeaway (max 5)
4. **Session Character**: Classify the session into exactly one of these types based on its overall nature:
   - deep_focus, bug_hunt, feature_build, exploration, refactor, learning, quick_task
   [existing descriptions unchanged]

[Quality Standards section unchanged]
[Length Guidance section unchanged]
[DO NOT include section unchanged]
[EXCELLENT examples section unchanged]

Respond with valid JSON only, wrapped in <json>...</json> tags.
```

**New output JSON schema** (in `generateSessionAnalysisPrompt`):

```json
{
  "session_character": "deep_focus | bug_hunt | feature_build | exploration | refactor | learning | quick_task",
  "summary": {
    "title": "Brief title describing main accomplishment (max 80 chars)",
    "content": "2-4 sentence narrative",
    "outcome": "success | partial | abandoned | blocked",
    "bullets": ["Each bullet names a specific artifact and what changed"]
  },
  "decisions": [
    {
      "title": "...",
      "situation": "...",
      "choice": "...",
      "reasoning": "...",
      "alternatives": [{"option": "...", "rejected_because": "..."}],
      "trade_offs": "...",
      "revisit_when": "...",
      "confidence": 85,
      "evidence": ["User#4: ...", "Assistant#5: ..."]
    }
  ],
  "learnings": [
    {
      "title": "...",
      "symptom": "...",
      "root_cause": "...",
      "takeaway": "...",
      "applies_when": "...",
      "confidence": 80,
      "evidence": ["User#7: ..."]
    }
  ]
}
```

**Token impact:** Removing friction/pattern guidance from this prompt saves approximately ~1,600 input tokens per session analysis call. The system prompt drops from ~2,200 tokens to ~600 tokens.

### 4b. PQ Prompt — What to Add

**Fields being added to PQ output:**
- `session_character`: one of 7 enum values
- `facets.outcome_satisfaction`: "high" | "medium" | "low" | "abandoned"
- `facets.workflow_pattern`: string | null
- `facets.had_course_correction`: boolean
- `facets.course_correction_reason`: string | null
- `facets.iteration_count`: number
- `facets.friction_points[]`: array with `_reasoning`, `category`, `attribution`, `description`, `severity`, `resolution`
- `facets.effective_patterns[]`: array with `_reasoning`, `category`, `description`, `confidence`, `driver`

**Guidance constants that move in:**
- `FRICTION_CLASSIFICATION_GUIDANCE` (~550 tokens)
- `EFFECTIVE_PATTERN_CLASSIFICATION_GUIDANCE` (~800 tokens)
- Session character classification descriptions (~200 tokens)

**System prompt persona change:**

The critical change is removing the instruction "Focus on USER messages only" (line 715 of current `prompts.ts`). The PQ prompt must now analyze the full conversation (both user and assistant behavior) to extract friction points and effective patterns, which are inherently about the interaction dynamics between user and AI.

Replace:
```
- Focus on USER messages only — don't critique the assistant's responses
```

With:
```
- For PROMPT QUALITY (takeaways, findings, dimension_scores): Focus on USER messages — evaluate how the user communicated with the AI
- For SESSION DYNAMICS (friction_points, effective_patterns, outcome, workflow): Analyze the FULL conversation — both user and assistant messages, tool calls, and tool results
```

**New PQ system prompt** (conceptual):

```
You are a prompt engineering coach AND session dynamics analyst helping developers improve their AI coding workflows. You review conversations to:
1. Identify specific moments where better prompting would have saved time — AND moments where the user prompted particularly well (PROMPT QUALITY)
2. Assess the session holistically — what friction occurred, what techniques worked, and how the session went overall (SESSION DYNAMICS)

PART 1 — SESSION DYNAMICS (extract these first as a holistic session assessment):

1. session_character: Classify the session into exactly one of these types:
   - deep_focus, bug_hunt, feature_build, exploration, refactor, learning, quick_task
   [descriptions from current session analysis prompt]

2. outcome_satisfaction: Rate the session outcome.
   - "high": Task completed successfully, user satisfied
   - "medium": Partial completion or minor issues
   - "low": Significant problems, user frustrated
   - "abandoned": Session ended without achieving the goal

3. workflow_pattern: Identify the dominant workflow pattern (or null).
   Values: "plan-then-implement", "iterative-refinement", "debug-fix-verify", "explore-then-build", "direct-execution"

4. friction_points: Up to 5 moments where progress was blocked or slowed.
   Each: { _reasoning, category, attribution, description, severity, resolution }
   [FRICTION_CLASSIFICATION_GUIDANCE injected here]

5. effective_patterns: Up to 3 techniques or approaches that worked particularly well.
   Each: { _reasoning, category, description, confidence, driver }
   [EFFECTIVE_PATTERN_CLASSIFICATION_GUIDANCE injected here]

6. had_course_correction: true/false
7. course_correction_reason: Brief explanation if true, null otherwise
8. iteration_count: Number of user clarification/correction cycles

PART 2 — PROMPT QUALITY:

You will produce:
1. Takeaways: Concrete before/after examples (max 4)
2. Findings: Categorized findings for cross-session aggregation (max 8)
3. Dimension scores: 5 numeric dimensions
4. Efficiency score: 0-100 overall rating
5. Assessment: 2-3 sentence summary

[PROMPT_QUALITY_CLASSIFICATION_GUIDANCE injected here]

SCOPE RULES:
- For PROMPT QUALITY (takeaways, findings, dimension_scores): Focus on USER messages — evaluate how the user communicated with the AI
- For SESSION DYNAMICS (friction_points, effective_patterns, outcome, workflow): Analyze the FULL conversation — both user and assistant messages, tool calls, and tool results
- Be constructive, not judgmental
- If the session has minimal friction and straightforward execution, use empty arrays for friction_points
- Include BOTH deficits and strengths in prompt quality — what went right matters as much as what went wrong

Length Guidance:
- Max 4 takeaways, max 8 findings, max 5 friction points, max 3 effective patterns
- Total response: stay under 3500 tokens
- Prefer precision over brevity

Respond with valid JSON only, wrapped in <json>...</json> tags.
```

**New output JSON schema (full merged schema):**

```json
{
  "session_character": "deep_focus | bug_hunt | feature_build | exploration | refactor | learning | quick_task",
  "facets": {
    "outcome_satisfaction": "high | medium | low | abandoned",
    "workflow_pattern": "plan-then-implement | iterative-refinement | debug-fix-verify | explore-then-build | direct-execution | null",
    "had_course_correction": false,
    "course_correction_reason": null,
    "iteration_count": 0,
    "friction_points": [
      {
        "_reasoning": "...",

      }

**Token budget estimate for the expanded PQ prompt:**

| Component | Current (tokens) | After (tokens) |
|-----------|-----------------|----------------|
| System prompt text | ~600 | ~400 (original PQ text) |
| Session dynamics section (outcome, workflow, character) | 0 | ~350 |
| FRICTION_CLASSIFICATION_GUIDANCE | 0 | ~550 |
| EFFECTIVE_PATTERN_CLASSIFICATION_GUIDANCE | 0 | ~800 |
| PROMPT_QUALITY_CLASSIFICATION_GUIDANCE | ~1,050 | ~1,050 |
| **Total system prompt** | **~2,250** | **~3,150** |
| User prompt template | ~400 | ~600 (expanded schema) |
| Conversation content | variable | variable (unchanged) |
| **Expected output** | ~800-1,500 | ~1,500-2,500 |
| **Total per call (excl. conversation)** | **~3,450-4,150** | **~5,250-6,250** |

Net token increase: ~1,800-2,100 additional tokens per PQ call. However, the Session Insights prompt drops ~1,600 tokens, so the **net system-wide change is ~+200-500 tokens per session** (both calls combined).

## 6. Backfill Strategy

### `extractFacetsOnly()` — unchanged, stays as legacy

`extractFacetsOnly()` continues to exist and serve two purposes:
1. **Backfill for sessions with no facets at all** — sessions that were analyzed before facets existed
2. **Facet extraction for chunked sessions** — wait, this is being removed from `analyzeSession()`. See below.

With this change, `analyzeSession()` no longer calls the facet-only prompt for chunked sessions. This means chunked sessions will NOT get facets from the Session Insights pipeline. Facets for chunked sessions will come from the PQ pipeline when `analyzePromptQuality()` is called. If PQ analysis is never triggered for a chunked session, facets will be missing.

**Recommendation:** This is acceptable because:
1. The dashboard already handles missing facets (shows a "Generate" button)
2. Backfill can catch these sessions later
3. In practice, users typically run both analysis pipelines

### CLI `reflect backfill` command

- NO CHANGE to the backfill command itself. It calls `POST /api/facets/backfill` which uses `extractFacetsOnly()`.
- A future enhancement could add a `--prompt-quality` flag that runs the full PQ analysis instead of the lightweight facet-only prompt. This would produce both facets AND PQ insights. **This is out of scope for this PR** — note it as follow-up.

### What happens for sessions with old-format facets?

Sessions that already have facets written by the old Session Insights pipeline will continue to work. The `session_facets` table schema is unchanged, and the data format is identical. No migration needed.

If a user re-runs PQ analysis on such a session, the new PQ pipeline will overwrite the facets via `INSERT OR REPLACE` (keyed on `session_id`). This is correct behavior — newer analysis supersedes older analysis.

## 7. Migration Path

### Single PR — yes

This can and should be done in a single PR. The changes are:
1. **Prompt text changes** in `prompts.ts` (~150 lines of moves/edits, no new logic)
2. **Analysis pipeline changes** in `analysis.ts` (~30 lines: remove facet saving from `analyzeSession`, add facet saving to `analyzePromptQuality`)
3. **Type changes** in `prompts.ts` (~10 lines: add facets to `PromptQualityResponse`)
4. **Monitor moves** in `prompts.ts` (~20 lines: move warn statements between parsers)

Total estimated diff: ~200 lines changed across 2 files.

### Backward compatibility

- **Existing facets:** Untouched. `session_facets` rows written by the old pipeline remain valid.
- **Existing insights:** Untouched. Summary, decision, learning rows remain valid. PQ insights remain valid.
- **Mixed state:** After deployment, some sessions will have facets from the old pipeline and some from the new. This is fine — the data format is identical.
- **Re-analysis:** Running "Analyze" on the dashboard triggers `analyzeSession()` which no longer writes facets. Running "Prompt Quality" triggers `analyzePromptQuality()` which now writes facets. If a user only runs "Analyze" and not "Prompt Quality," that session will not get new facets. Existing facets (if any) are preserved since `analyzeSession()` no longer calls `saveFacetsToDb()` at all.
- **Dashboard UX consideration:** If the dashboard auto-triggers both analyses, no change needed. If it triggers them independently, the user may need to run PQ analysis to get facets for new sessions. The dashboard already shows a "missing facets" indicator, so this is self-correcting.

### Outdated detection

No new outdated detection arm is needed for this migration. Existing facets have all the required fields (category, driver, attribution, _reasoning). The outdated detection continues to flag sessions missing these structural fields regardless of which pipeline produced them.

### Version bump

Bump `ANALYSIS_VERSION` from `'3.0.0'` to `'4.0.0'` in `analysis.ts` line 37. Both `analyzeSession()` (via `convertToInsightRows`) and `saveFacetsToDb()` use this constant. After the change, new facets written by `analyzePromptQuality()` will have `analysis_version = '4.0.0'`, distinguishing them from old-pipeline facets if needed for future queries.

## 8. Risk Assessment

### What could go wrong

**R1: PQ prompt produces lower-quality friction/pattern data than Session Insights prompt.**
The Session Insights prompt had the persona of a "senior staff engineer" optimized for extracting technical dynamics. The PQ prompt has a "prompt coaching" persona. The friction/pattern extraction quality may differ.
- **Mitigation:** The expanded PQ system prompt explicitly separates the two roles with a "PART 1 — SESSION DYNAMICS" section and clear scope rules. The classification guidance constants (FRICTION_CLASSIFICATION_GUIDANCE, EFFECTIVE_PATTERN_CLASSIFICATION_GUIDANCE) are identical.
- **Monitoring:** The existing `[friction-monitor]`, `[pattern-monitor]`, and `[cot-monitor]` console.warn statements (moved to `parsePromptQualityResponse`) will flag quality issues.

**R2: Token budget overflow on large sessions.**
The PQ prompt now has ~900 more input tokens (system prompt growth). For sessions near the `MAX_INPUT_TOKENS` limit, this reduces available space for conversation content.
- **Mitigation:** The existing truncation logic in `analyzePromptQuality()` (lines 323-327) already handles this. The truncation point shifts slightly earlier, losing maybe 500 chars of conversation tail. Not significant.

**R3: Sessions get facets only when PQ analysis is run.**
If a user runs Session Insights but not PQ analysis, that session will have insights but no facets. The "missing facets" count in the dashboard will increase.
- **Mitigation:** The dashboard's facet backfill UI handles this. The `extractFacetsOnly()` backfill path is unchanged.
- **Alternative:** Have `analyzeSession()` also call `analyzePromptQuality()` automatically. But this doubles LLM cost and is a UX decision, not an architecture decision. **Recommend leaving as-is and evaluating whether the dashboard should auto-trigger PQ after session analysis.**

**R4: Small Ollama models may struggle with the larger PQ prompt.**
The PQ prompt grows from ~2,250 to ~3,150 system prompt tokens. Models like Llama 3.1 8B may produce lower quality output with the expanded instructions.
- **Mitigation:** The classification guidance includes contrastive pairs and decision trees that are designed to help smaller models. The `_reasoning` CoT scratchpad forces step-by-step classification.
- **Monitoring:** Existing monitors will catch quality degradation.

**R5: `analyzePromptQuality()` has a `userMessages.length < 2` guard that prevents analysis of very short sessions.**
Currently (line 311), PQ analysis is skipped if there are fewer than 2 user messages. This means short sessions would also lose facets.
- **Mitigation:** This guard protects against meaningless PQ analysis, but facets can still be valuable for short sessions. Options:
  - Lower the guard to 1 user message
  - Add a separate facet-extraction path for sessions that fail the guard
  - Accept that very short sessions (< 2 user messages) won't get facets unless backfilled
- **Recommendation:** Lower guard to 1 user message OR make the guard only skip PQ-specific outputs (takeaways, findings, scores) while still extracting facets. **This needs user input.**

### Rollback plan

1. Revert the PR. No data migration is needed — existing data is untouched.
2. Sessions analyzed during the rollout window will have:
   - Insights (summary, decisions, learnings) without facets — if only Session Insights was run
   - Facets + PQ insight — if PQ analysis was run
3. After revert, running Session Insights again will re-create facets for sessions missing them.

### Model compatibility concerns

The expanded PQ prompt stays within comfortable limits for all supported models:
- GPT-4o, Claude 3.5/4, Gemini 2: ~3,150 system prompt tokens is well within capacity
- Llama 3.1 70B (Ollama): handles this size fine
- Llama 3.1 8B (Ollama): may produce noisier friction/pattern classifications. The existing monitors will detect this. **Pre-existing risk** — this model already struggles with the current Session Insights prompt's friction/pattern extraction.

## 9. Open Questions

**Q1: Should `analyzeSession()` auto-trigger `analyzePromptQuality()` to ensure facets are always generated?**
This would double the LLM cost per "Analyze" click but guarantee facets are never missing. Currently the dashboard may need to guide users to run PQ analysis separately.

**Q2: What should happen with the `userMessages.length < 2` guard in `analyzePromptQuality()`?**
Options: (a) lower to 1, (b) skip PQ outputs but still extract facets, (c) leave as-is and accept short sessions won't get facets. See R5 above.

**Q3: Should the dashboard "Analyze" button trigger both pipelines in sequence?**
This is a UX question. If yes, the dashboard code needs to chain two API calls. If no, the "missing facets" indicator handles it. Current implementation triggers them independently.

**Q4: Should the facet-only backfill prompt (`FACET_ONLY_SYSTEM_PROMPT`) be updated to match the PQ prompt's friction/pattern guidance?**
Currently the facet-only prompt already includes the same guidance constants. No change is strictly needed. But its persona is "assessing an AI coding session to extract structured metadata" — it does not have the PQ coaching persona. This is fine because backfill only extracts facets, not PQ insights.

**Q5: Should chunked sessions get special handling?**
Currently, chunked sessions get facets via a separate facet-only LLM call within `analyzeSession()`. With this change, that call is removed. Chunked sessions will rely on PQ analysis (or backfill) for facets. Is this acceptable, or should `analyzeSession()` still call the facet-only prompt for chunked sessions as a fallback?

Now I have the full picture. Currently:
- **Session analysis** (`analyzeSession`) generates insights (summary, decisions, learnings, techniques) AND facets (friction, patterns, outcome, workflow)
- **PQ analysis** (`analyzePromptQuality`) is a separate, optional call that generates prompt quality findings

The proposed restructuring would move facets OUT of session analysis and INTO PQ analysis.

---

## Cofounder Design Review: Prompt Pipeline Restructuring

### 1. Product Wins

**Cleaner separation of concerns in the product narrative.** Right now, "Analyze Session" does two conceptually distinct things: knowledge capture (decisions, learnings) AND behavioral analysis (friction, patterns, workflow). Splitting these makes each button's promise clearer. Users clicking "Analyze Session" want their knowledge captured. Users clicking the behavioral analysis want coaching.

**Stronger PQ value proposition.** The current PQ is a nice-to-have that tells you "your prompts were 72/100." By bundling friction points, effective patterns, and session dynamics into it, PQ becomes the engine that powers Reflect. That transforms it from a novelty into something with compounding value -- each PQ analysis feeds into your weekly patterns page, making the data richer over time.

**Architectural honesty.** Friction points and effective patterns ARE session dynamics -- they describe how the session went, who contributed what, where things went sideways. That belongs with the behavioral analysis (PQ), not with knowledge capture (insights). The current bundling was expedient, not intentional.

### 2. UX Concerns

**This is a breaking change to the core user journey, and the plan doesn't acknowledge it.** Today, a user clicks "Analyze" on a session and gets everything: insights for their notes AND facets for their Reflect page. After this change, that same user clicks "Analyze," sees their insights, goes to the Patterns page, and sees... nothing new. That is a regression in time-to-value. The user did what they always did and got less. This will feel broken.

**The naming problem is real and severe.** "Prompt Quality" implies a narrow coaching lens -- "were my prompts good?" But now it also extracts friction points (which might be AI-capability or environmental -- nothing to do with prompting), effective patterns (which might be AI-driven), session outcome, workflow type, and course corrections. Calling all of that "Prompt Quality" is misleading. Users will skip it thinking "I don't care about prompt scores" and miss the friction/pattern data they actually want.

**The "two-button tax" is a real problem.** Every devtool that requires two clicks to get the full picture loses users at the second click. Look at how Sentry bundles performance + errors -- they learned the hard way that developers will NOT manually trigger a second analysis pass. The question is not "is two buttons cleaner architecturally?" -- it is "will users actually click both?" History says no, most will not.

**Cost perception shifts unfavorably.** Currently: "Analyze" = one LLM call, gives you everything. PQ = optional bonus. After this change: "Analyze" = one LLM call, gives you half. "Session Review" (or whatever we rename it) = second LLM call, gives you the other half. Users now need two LLM calls per session to get what they used to get in one. That is a real cost increase. For users on metered API keys (everyone -- this is BYOK), that matters.

**Backfill transition is jarring.** Old sessions have facets from "Analyze." New sessions need PQ for facets. The Reflect page will show a gap. The "outdated facets" detection will fire. Users will see "N sessions need re-analysis" but the fix is now "run PQ on those sessions" not "re-analyze." This is confusing without very explicit messaging about what changed and why.

### 3. Recommendations

**Do not split the pipeline. Expand PQ's scope WITHOUT removing facets from session analysis.**

Here is the specific recommendation:

**Option A: Dual-write (recommended).** Keep facets in session analysis AND add the expanded behavioral data to PQ. Session analysis produces "baseline" facets. PQ produces "enriched" facets that overwrite the baseline. This means:
- Users who only click "Analyze" still get facets and their Reflect page still works.
- Users who also run PQ get richer, more nuanced facets (with the PQ-specific attribution model, dimensional scores, etc.).
- No regression, no two-button tax, no breaking change.
- PQ becomes a "enhance my analysis" action rather than a "complete my analysis" action.

The precedent here is Sentry's performance monitoring: you get basic metrics from the error SDK, but enabling the Performance product gives you richer data. The basic version still works.

**Option B: Single "Full Analysis" button.** If you truly want one pipeline per concern, then merge the buttons. One click fires both LLM calls in sequence (or parallel). Show unified progress: "Analyzing session... Assessing prompt quality..." The user sees one action, two things happen under the hood. This is how Cursor's "Analyze" works -- it does multiple things, but the user clicks once.

Option A is better because it preserves backward compatibility and makes PQ a value-add rather than a requirement.

**On naming:** If you do expand PQ's scope (regardless of pipeline), rename it. "Prompt Quality" no longer describes what it does. Suggestions:
- **"Session Review"** -- broad enough to cover prompt quality + friction + patterns + dynamics. Mirrors code review (you review the session, not just the prompts).
- **"Session Coaching"** -- implies learning and improvement, which is the actual intent.
- **"Deep Analysis"** -- positions it as the enhanced version of basic "Analyze."

Do NOT call it "Session Dynamics" or "Behavioral Analysis" -- those are internal architecture terms, not user-facing concepts.

**On the dashboard flow:** Whatever you do, the Patterns/Reflect page needs to show users what is missing and how to fix it. If facets require PQ, the Patterns page should have a clear CTA: "Run Session Review on 12 sessions to unlock pattern insights" with a bulk action button. Do not make users navigate to each session individually. The BulkAnalyzeButton pattern already exists -- extend it.

### 4. Positioning Impact

**The current narrative is clean:** "Analyze your AI sessions. See patterns over time." One action, compounding value.

**The proposed split muddies it:** "Analyze your sessions for knowledge. Then also run prompt quality for behavioral analysis. Both feed into patterns." That is two concepts, two actions, two explanations in the README.

**With Option A (dual-write), the narrative improves:**
- "Analyze" = capture what you built, decided, and learned (knowledge capture)
- "Session Review" = understand how the session went and how to improve (coaching)
- Both feed into Reflect, but Analyze alone gets you started
- Session Review deepens the analysis with prompt-specific feedback and richer facet data

This positions Code Insights as having a natural progression: sync -> analyze -> (optionally) review -> reflect. Progressive disclosure. The user gets value at each step. No step is required to unlock the next, but each step enriches it.

**For the README / marketing site:**
> "Every analysis captures your session's friction points and effective patterns. Run Session Review for deeper coaching on your prompting technique and enriched behavioral data."

That is a cleaner story than "you need to run two separate analyses to get the full picture."

---

**Bottom line:** The architectural separation makes sense in theory, but the UX cost is too high. Dual-write (Option A) gets you the clean pipeline separation you want without breaking the user journey. PQ becomes an enhancer, not a prerequisite. That is the devtools pattern that works -- Sentry, DataDog, Grafana all follow it. Give baseline value for free, let users opt into deeper analysis.

I have all the data I need. Here is my review.

---

## TA Design Review: Prompt Pipeline Restructuring

### 1. VALIDATED -- Things the Design Got Right

**1a. `saveFacetsToDb()` uses `INSERT OR REPLACE` -- CONFIRMED.**
Line 859 of `server/src/llm/analysis.ts` uses `INSERT OR REPLACE INTO session_facets`. The `session_facets` table has `session_id TEXT PRIMARY KEY` (migrate.ts line 65). This means any write to `session_facets` for the same session_id fully replaces the previous row. The design is correct that PQ can safely overwrite facets previously written by session insights.

**1b. DB schema stays identical -- CONFIRMED.**
No schema changes are needed. `session_facets` stores facets keyed by `session_id`; it doesn't care which prompt produced the data.

**1c. Chunked session facet flow exists and is separable.**
Lines 172-211 show the chunked-session fallback that calls `generateFacetOnlyPrompt()`. This code block is cleanly isolated inside the `if (estimatedTokens > MAX_INPUT_TOKENS)` branch. Removing it is a surgical operation.

**1d. `ANALYSIS_VERSION` is a simple string constant.**
Line 37: `const ANALYSIS_VERSION = '3.0.0'`. It's stamped onto every insight row and every `session_facets` row. Bumping it is trivial.

---

### 2. CONCERNS -- Things That Need Attention

**C1. Race condition: concurrent writes to `session_facets` (MEDIUM RISK).**

Currently `analyzeSession()` and `analyzePromptQuality()` are completely independent -- they write to different tables (`insights` with different type filters, and only `analyzeSession()` writes to `session_facets`). After this change, BOTH could write to `session_facets` if a user triggers them in sequence or (theoretically) in parallel.

The dashboard `AnalysisContext.tsx` (line 107) has a guard: `if (isAnalyzingRef.current)` prevents concurrent analysis from the UI. However, the API routes themselves have NO concurrency guard -- two direct POST calls to `/api/analysis/session` and `/api/analysis/prompt-quality` with the same sessionId could run simultaneously.

In practice this is low risk because: (a) better-sqlite3 is synchronous and serializes writes internally, (b) both use `INSERT OR REPLACE` which is atomic per row, and (c) the last writer wins cleanly. But the semantic concern is: if session insights analysis finishes AFTER PQ analysis, it would overwrite PQ's facets with nothing (since after this change, session insights no longer produces facets).

Wait -- that's actually the critical point. After this restructuring, `analyzeSession()` should STOP calling `saveFacetsToDb()` entirely. If the implementation removes lines 257-259 from `analyzeSession()` (the `if (analysisResponse.facets)` block), then there's no conflict. But if it's not removed, you get a silent overwrite scenario where stale/partial facets from the session insights call clobber the PQ-generated facets.

**Recommendation:** The design must be explicit: lines 257-259 AND lines 172-211 are BOTH removed from `analyzeSession()`. Not just one.

**C2. The `userMessages.length < 2` guard creates a facet gap (HIGH IMPORTANCE).**

Currently (lines 310-317), `analyzePromptQuality()` returns early with an error if fewer than 2 user messages exist. After this restructuring, PQ becomes the SOLE source of facets. Sessions with 0-1 user messages will NEVER get facets. This is a real gap.

Looking at the data: single-message sessions are common (quick one-shot questions). These sessions currently get facets via the session insights prompt. After this change, they lose facets entirely.

The `extractFacetsOnly()` function (line 882) has NO such guard -- it works with any number of messages. This is the existing backfill path and would still work, but it requires the user to manually run `reflect backfill`.

**C3. Backfill implications are NOT as clean as stated.**

The design says `extractFacetsOnly()` stays unchanged. That's technically true -- the function itself doesn't change. But the MEANING changes significantly:

- **Before:** `extractFacetsOnly()` is a backfill tool for sessions that were analyzed before facets existed. It's lightweight (first/last 20 messages only).
- **After:** `extractFacetsOnly()` becomes the ONLY way to get facets for sessions with <2 user messages. But it uses the old FACET_ONLY prompt, which may not include the latest taxonomy (attribution, driver, etc.) -- need to verify.

More importantly, the `reflect backfill` command currently finds sessions via `/api/facets/missing` (sessions with insights but no facets row) and `/api/facets/outdated` (facets missing attribution/driver/category). After this change, sessions analyzed ONLY via session insights (not PQ) would appear as "missing" in the facets/missing query, which is correct behavior. But a user who only runs "Analyze Session" and never runs "Analyze Prompt Quality" would have NO facets for that session. The backfill command would then use the lightweight facet-only prompt for those sessions, which is architecturally fine but gives lower quality facets than the full PQ prompt would have.

**C4. Dashboard "Analyze Session" no longer produces facets -- user expectation mismatch.**

Currently, clicking "Analyze Session" in the dropdown (AnalyzeDropdown.tsx line 144) produces insights AND facets. After this change, it produces ONLY insights (summary, decisions, learnings). The Reflect page would show no friction/patterns data until the user ALSO clicks "Analyze Prompt Quality."

This is a UX concern, not a technical one, but it has architectural implications: should the session analysis route automatically chain to PQ analysis? Or should the dashboard be updated to run both?

---

### 3. RECOMMENDATIONS -- Answers to Open Questions

**R1. The `userMessages.length < 2` guard (design doc's R5).**

My recommendation: **Keep the guard for the PQ-specific outputs** (efficiency_score, assessment, takeaways, findings, dimension_scores) but **still extract facets** for sessions with <2 user messages.

Implementation approach: In `analyzePromptQuality()`, when `userMessages.length < 2`, skip the PQ-specific analysis but still run a facet extraction. Either call `extractFacetsOnly()` internally, or produce a subset response with facets only and null PQ scores.

This preserves the data contract: every analyzed session gets facets, regardless of user message count.

**R2. `ANALYSIS_VERSION` bump to 4.0.0.**

`ANALYSIS_VERSION` is stamped on both `insights.analysis_version` and `session_facets.analysis_version`. It serves as a data provenance marker. Bumping to 4.0.0 is appropriate because:
- The source of facets changes (session prompt to PQ prompt)
- The prompt content changes significantly
- Old facets (version 3.0.0) were generated by a different prompt

This enables the `/api/facets/outdated` endpoint to be extended later to flag sessions with `analysis_version < '4.0.0'` if needed for re-processing.

However, note a subtlety: after this change, `analyzeSession()` stamps insights with the new version but does NOT write facets. `analyzePromptQuality()` stamps BOTH insights and facets with the same version. If you want to distinguish "facets from PQ" vs "facets from old session prompt" vs "facets from backfill," a single version bump is insufficient. I'd suggest: keep `ANALYSIS_VERSION = '4.0.0'` as a single constant, but document that version 4.0.0 facets come from PQ, while version 3.0.0 facets come from the old session prompt.

**R3. Should `reflect backfill` trigger PQ analysis?**

No. Keep `reflect backfill` using `extractFacetsOnly()`. The backfill path is designed for bulk processing (up to 200 sessions) with a lightweight prompt. Running full PQ analysis for 200 sessions would be expensive (5-10x more tokens per session). The facet-only prompt is purpose-built for this use case.

But DO update the dashboard and/or CLI to make it obvious that "Analyze Prompt Quality" is the recommended way to get facets for individual sessions going forward.

---

### 4. RISK ADDITIONS -- Things the LLM Expert May Have Missed

**R-A. Token budget increase for PQ prompt.**

The PQ prompt currently handles: efficiency_score, assessment, takeaways, findings, dimension_scores. After this change, it ALSO handles: outcome_satisfaction, workflow_pattern, course_correction, iteration_count, friction_points, effective_patterns. That's roughly doubling the structured output. The output token budget needs to increase accordingly, and the system prompt needs to instruct the model to produce this combined schema.

Risk: if the output is too large for the model's output window, it may truncate -- resulting in partial JSON that fails to parse, or silently drops facets. The current `parsePromptQualityResponse()` would need to handle the combined schema.

**R-B. `parsedPromptQualityResponse()` must be updated to extract facets.**

Currently `parsePromptQualityResponse()` in prompts.ts returns a `PromptQualityResponse` type. This function would need to also extract and return facets. The `analyzePromptQuality()` function would then need to call `saveFacetsToDb()` after saving the PQ insight. This is the core implementation work -- the design should specify the exact shape of the combined response type.

**R-C. Outdated detection query needs updating.**

The `/api/facets/outdated` endpoint (facets.ts lines 135-152) flags sessions where facets lack `category`, `driver`, or `attribution`. After this change, sessions analyzed with the OLD pipeline (version 3.0.0) will have facets but they were generated by the session insights prompt, not PQ. Should these be flagged as outdated? If yes, add an `analysis_version < '4.0.0'` arm to the UNION query.

**R-D. `analyzeSession()` still has `facets` in its `AnalysisResponse` type.**

Even after removing facet extraction from `analyzeSession()`, the `AnalysisResponse` type in prompts.ts likely still has a `facets` field. The session analysis LLM prompt still asks for facets. Both the prompt and the type need to be updated to remove the facets request, otherwise the LLM wastes tokens generating facets that are thrown away.

**R-E. The `mergeAnalysisResponses()` function currently discards facets from chunked responses (line 593-608).**

This is already a known limitation (the comment on line 173 says "facets are holistic -- can't be merged across chunks"). After this change, this is no longer a concern since session analysis won't produce facets at all. But it validates the design's reasoning: facets SHOULD come from a holistic view of the session, which the PQ prompt gets (it sees the full conversation, or a truncated version for long sessions).

**R-F. Error handling gap: what if PQ analysis succeeds for insights but fails for facets?**

Currently in `analyzeSession()`, facet saving failure is silent (line 257-259, inside a simple `if`). After this change, facets are part of the PQ response. If `saveFacetsToDb()` throws (e.g., invalid data), the entire PQ analysis would fail. Consider wrapping the facet save in a try-catch within `analyzePromptQuality()` so PQ insights are saved even if facet extraction/parsing fails.

---

### Summary

The architectural direction is sound -- consolidating facets into the PQ prompt makes sense because the PQ prompt already sees the full conversation and is better positioned to evaluate friction and patterns in context of user behavior. The main risks are:

1. **Gap for sessions with <2 user messages** (they lose facets entirely -- needs a fallback)
2. **Dashboard UX: "Analyze Session" no longer produces facets** (users may not know to also run PQ)
3. **Token budget and response schema changes** for the combined PQ+facets output
4. **Clean removal of facet code from BOTH paths** in `analyzeSession()` (lines 172-211 AND 257-259)

These are addressable concerns, not blockers. The design should be updated to address items C2 and C4 before implementation proceeds.

Both agents delivered strong, distinct perspectives. Let me synthesize:

---

## Technical Architect — Key Findings

**Validated:**
- `saveFacetsToDb()` uses `INSERT OR REPLACE` — PQ can safely overwrite existing facets
- DB schema stays identical, no migration needed
- Chunked session facet code is cleanly isolatable

**Critical Concerns:**

| # | Concern | Severity |
|---|---------|----------|
| C1 | Both `analyzeSession()` paths (lines 172-211 AND 257-259) must be removed — not just one | Implementation detail |
| C2 | **`userMessages.length < 2` guard blocks facets for short sessions** — single-message sessions are common and currently get facets via session insights. After this change, they lose facets entirely | High |
| C3 | Backfill meaning changes — `extractFacetsOnly()` becomes the ONLY way to get facets for <2 message sessions | Medium |
| C4 | Dashboard "Analyze" no longer produces facets — user expectation mismatch | High (UX) |

**TA's recommendation for the guard:** Keep the guard for PQ-specific outputs (scores, findings, takeaways) but still extract facets for sessions with <2 user messages — either via `extractFacetsOnly()` internally, or a subset response.

---

## Devtools Cofounder — Key Findings

**Product wins:** Cleaner separation, stronger PQ value proposition, architectural honesty.

**But — strong pushback on the UX:**

> *"This is a breaking change to the core user journey. Today a user clicks 'Analyze' and gets everything. After this change, they click 'Analyze', go to Patterns, and see... nothing new. That will feel broken."*

> *"Every devtool that requires two clicks to get the full picture loses users at the second click."*

**The Cofounder's counter-proposal — Option A: Dual-Write:**

- **Keep facets in session analysis** (baseline facets)
- **Also add enriched facets to PQ** (overwrite baseline when PQ runs)
- Users who only click "Analyze" still get facets and Reflect still works
- Users who also run PQ get richer, more nuanced facets
- **No regression, no two-button tax, no breaking change**
- PQ becomes an "enhance my analysis" action, not a "complete my analysis" action

**On naming:** "Prompt Quality" no longer describes what PQ does if it also extracts friction/patterns. Suggestions: **"Session Review"**, "Session Coaching", or "Deep Analysis."

---

## The Core Tension

| Approach | Clean separation? | UX regression? | Cost? |
|----------|------------------|----------------|-------|
| **LLM Expert's plan** (full split) | Yes | Yes — "Analyze" gives less than before | Same (2 calls) |
| **Cofounder's Option A** (dual-write) | Partial — facets generated twice | No — baseline always available | Same (2 calls), PQ overwrites |
| **Cofounder's Option B** (single button) | Yes | No — one click fires both | 2 calls always (higher cost) |

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
The Cofounder's dual-write pattern mirrors how Sentry/Datadog work: basic metrics from the lightweight SDK, enriched data when you enable the premium product. The baseline version always works. The enhanced version overwrites with richer data. This is a proven devtools pattern because it avoids the "cliff" where users get LESS from an action they've already learned to trust.

The TA's concern about the `<2 user messages` guard reinforces this — dual-write elegantly solves it. Short sessions get baseline facets from session insights. Longer sessions get enriched facets from PQ.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

---

**This is a product decision that shapes the user experience. Three options on the table:**

1. **Full split** (LLM Expert's plan) — cleanest architecture, but "Analyze" gives less than before
2. **Dual-write** (Cofounder's Option A) — session insights produces baseline facets, PQ overwrites with enriched facets. No regression. PQ is an enhancer, not a prerequisite.
3. **Single button** (Cofounder's Option B) — one "Analyze" click fires both calls sequentially. Clean UX but always costs 2 LLM calls

## LLM Output Quality Analysis: Dual-Write Pattern

### Q1: Quality Divergence — Will the Two Prompts Produce Different Friction/Patterns?

**Yes, and the divergence will be systematic, not random.**

The two prompts have fundamentally different analytical lenses:

**Session Analysis prompt** (line 259): *"You are a senior staff engineer writing entries for a team's engineering knowledge base."* This persona evaluates the session holistically -- what happened, what worked, what blocked progress. Friction and patterns are assessed in the context of the overall engineering outcome.

**PQ prompt** (line 694): *"You are a prompt engineering coach helping developers communicate more effectively with AI coding assistants."* This persona is laser-focused on USER PROMPTING BEHAVIOR. It explicitly says "Focus on USER messages only -- don't critique the assistant's responses" (line 715).

The divergence will manifest in predictable ways:

1. **Friction category assignment**: The session analysis prompt's 3-step attribution tree starts with "Is the cause external?" then "Could the USER have prevented this?" then "User input was clear and the AI still failed." This is balanced. A PQ-expanded prompt would inherit the coaching lens, biasing Step 2. When the PQ persona sees a friction point, its instinct is to frame it as a prompting failure -- because that is its entire job. Example: an `ai-capability` friction (the AI misused an API despite clear instructions) might get re-classified as `missing-context` by the PQ prompt because the coaching persona thinks "well, the user could have provided the API docs." Both readings are defensible, but they will produce different `attribution` values 15-25% of the time.

2. **Attribution skew**: This is the highest-risk divergence. The PQ prompt's coaching orientation will systematically over-attribute friction to `user-actionable`. The session analysis prompt's engineering orientation produces more balanced attribution because it genuinely considers AI capability failures. The existing tiebreaker "lean user-actionable when mixed" (line 107) already nudges toward user attribution -- stacking a coaching persona on top of that nudge will amplify the effect.

3. **Effective pattern categories**: Less divergent here. Both prompts would use the same `EFFECTIVE_PATTERN_CLASSIFICATION_GUIDANCE` constant. However, the coaching persona is likely to under-report `ai-driven` patterns (self-correction, domain-expertise) because those are AI accomplishments, and the coach is looking at user behavior. The session analysis prompt's engineering persona genuinely notices when the AI did something impressive.

4. **Pattern driver assignment**: The 4-step driver decision tree starts with "Did user infrastructure enable this?" The PQ persona will be more aggressive about calling patterns `user-driven` because it wants to credit user behavior. Estimated divergence: 10-20% of driver assignments will differ.

**Bottom line on Q1**: Expect 15-25% divergence on attribution, 10-15% on friction categories (especially at ambiguous boundaries like `incomplete-requirements` vs `missing-context`), and 10-20% on pattern driver. This is not noise -- it is systematic bias from the persona difference.

---

### Q2: Does the Overwrite Improve or Degrade Quality?

**It will degrade quality for friction attribution. It will be neutral-to-slightly-worse for patterns.**

Here is the core issue: the session analysis prompt's friction/pattern pipeline has been refined across PRs #125, #127, #129, #136, and #138. Each iteration added:
- Actor-neutral category definitions (PR #138)
- 3-step CoT attribution decision tree with `_reasoning` scratchpad (PR #138)
- Baseline exclusion for patterns (PR #129/138)
- Contrastive pairs for disambiguation (PR #129)
- Collaborative tiebreaker (PR #129)

These refinements were designed FOR the session analysis persona. They work because the "senior staff engineer" persona approaches friction neutrally -- it genuinely asks "was this an AI failure or a user failure?" before classifying.

Transplanting the same guidance text into a coaching persona does not produce the same behavior. The guidance says "lean user-actionable when mixed" (line 107), but the coaching persona already has a prior toward user-actionable before it even reads that instruction. The tiebreaker becomes a thumb on a scale that was already tilted.

**The `INSERT OR REPLACE` overwrite is particularly dangerous because it is silent and irreversible.** When PQ facets overwrite session analysis facets:
- There is no diff, no log, no way to compare what changed
- If PQ attribution is systematically biased (and it will be), the Reflect aggregation downstream sees only the biased version
- Progress tracking filters on `attribution = 'user-actionable'` -- an inflated `user-actionable` count makes the user look worse than they are, which undermines trust in the tool's narrative

**The overwrite could make things worse for a specific scenario**: a session where the AI genuinely failed despite clear instructions. The session analysis prompt correctly classifies this as `ai-capability`. The PQ prompt, scanning the same conversation through a coaching lens, finds a way to frame it as `missing-context` or `assumption-not-surfaced`. The overwrite replaces a correct attribution with an incorrect one.

---

### Q3: Cognitive Load — Twice vs Once

**Doing the same analytical work twice on the same data is token waste with negative quality implications.**

Token cost: The `FRICTION_CLASSIFICATION_GUIDANCE` is ~550 tokens. The `EFFECTIVE_PATTERN_CLASSIFICATION_GUIDANCE` is ~650 tokens. Together, ~1,200 input tokens. Plus the output overhead of generating friction_points and effective_patterns JSON (~400-600 output tokens). That is ~1,800 tokens of duplicated work per session when both prompts run.

But the deeper issue is not cost -- it is that **the LLM has no memory of its own prior analysis**. When the PQ prompt generates friction/patterns, it does not know what the session analysis prompt already concluded. It cannot confirm, refine, or disagree with the prior analysis. It starts from scratch with a different persona. This is not "ensemble classification" (which would require comparing both outputs) -- it is "last writer wins," which discards the first analysis entirely.

If the goal were ensemble quality, you would want both outputs and a merge strategy. Dual-write with overwrite gives you the worst of both worlds: you pay for two analyses but keep only the second.

**Is there value in redundancy?** Only if you inspect both outputs and reconcile differences. The dual-write pattern as described does not do this -- it silently replaces. That makes it pure waste.

---

### Q4: Can Friction/Pattern Extraction Be Added to PQ Without Degrading PQ Quality?

**Yes, but with measurable quality dilution.**

The PQ prompt currently produces focused output: takeaways, findings, dimension_scores, efficiency_score, assessment. The system prompt is ~700 tokens (lines 694-728), the user prompt is ~450 tokens (lines 735-811), plus the classification guidance at ~1,050 tokens. Total PQ-specific input: ~2,200 tokens.

Adding friction/pattern guidance adds ~1,200 tokens of input and requires ~400-600 tokens of additional output. The prompt goes from ~2,200 to ~3,400 input tokens -- a 55% increase. The output schema goes from one focused concern (prompting quality) to three concerns (prompting quality + friction classification + pattern classification).

The attention dilution concern is real but model-dependent:
- **Claude 3.5 Sonnet / GPT-4o**: Can handle multi-concern prompts well at this scale. Expect 5-10% quality drop on PQ-specific output (takeaways become slightly less specific, dimension scores slightly less calibrated).
- **Smaller models (Ollama/Llama 3)**: Will struggle more. These models already have trouble with the existing PQ classification guidance. Adding friction/pattern guidance with its own decision trees and contrastive pairs will cause instruction interference. Expect 15-25% quality drop on PQ output.
- **The `_reasoning` CoT field compounds this**: Two separate `_reasoning` fields (one for friction, one for patterns) plus the PQ mental walkthrough (lines 703-710) means the model needs to maintain three separate chains of thought in one response. Smaller models will collapse these or skip some.

**The deeper issue is persona conflict.** The PQ prompt says "Focus on USER messages only" (line 715). The friction classification guidance says "Step 3: User input was clear and the AI still failed -> ai-capability" (line 106). These instructions are in tension. The PQ persona is told to focus on user behavior, but the friction guidance asks it to also evaluate AI behavior to determine attribution. A well-aligned model will notice this tension and produce awkward hedging. A weaker model will just ignore the friction guidance's Step 3 and classify everything as user-actionable.

---

### Q5: Honest Recommendation

**The current approach (friction/patterns only in session insights, PQ stays narrow) produces the best AI output quality today.** Here is why, and what I would actually recommend:

**Why dual-write is the wrong approach from a quality perspective:**
1. Systematic attribution bias from persona mismatch (Q1)
2. Silent overwrite destroys higher-quality session analysis facets (Q2)
3. ~1,800 tokens of duplicated work with no ensemble benefit (Q3)
4. PQ quality dilution, especially on smaller models (Q4)

**Why a clean split (friction/patterns only in PQ) is also risky:**
- The PQ prompt's coaching persona is the wrong lens for balanced friction attribution. You would need to rewrite the PQ system prompt to have a dual personality -- coach for PQ, neutral engineer for facets. Dual-personality prompts are fragile and produce inconsistent output.
- The session analysis prompt has been iteratively refined for this exact task. Moving the work to a different prompt means re-doing that tuning from scratch.

**My recommendation: Keep friction/patterns in the session analysis prompt. Do not duplicate them in PQ.**

If the architectural goal is to decouple "Analyze" and "PQ" so they can run independently, the cleanest approach from an LLM output quality perspective is:

1. **Session Analysis prompt**: Produces insights (summary, decisions, learnings, session_character) AND facets (friction, patterns, outcome, workflow, iterations, corrections). This is what it does today. It stays the single source of truth for facets.

2. **PQ prompt**: Produces ONLY prompt quality output (takeaways, findings, dimension_scores, efficiency_score, assessment). It stays focused. It stays good at what it does.

3. **No overwrite, no dual-write.** Each prompt owns its output domain exclusively.

If the concern is "what if the user only clicks PQ and never clicks Analyze?" -- that is a UX/architecture question, not a quality question. The answer is: make the PQ button trigger both prompts (session analysis first, PQ second), or make facets a prerequisite for PQ. Do not solve the sequencing problem by degrading the classification prompt.

If there is genuine interest in using the PQ analysis to ENRICH facets (e.g., PQ findings inform friction attribution), the right pattern is **chained prompts, not dual-write**: run session analysis first, then pass its facet output as additional context to the PQ prompt, and let PQ flag attribution disagreements as annotations rather than overwrites. But that is a more complex architecture and should only be pursued if there is evidence that session analysis attribution is systematically wrong in a way PQ could correct.

---

**Summary of quality impact ratings:**

| Dimension | Dual-Write | Clean Split (PQ owns facets) | Current (Session Analysis owns facets) |
|-----------|-----------|------------------------------|---------------------------------------|
| Friction category accuracy | Degraded (persona bias) | Degraded (wrong persona) | Best (tuned for this) |
| Attribution balance | Worst (coaching bias + overwrite) | Poor (coaching bias) | Best (neutral engineer) |
| Pattern driver accuracy | Slightly worse | Slightly worse | Best |
| PQ takeaway quality | Degraded (attention split) | Preserved | Preserved |
| Token efficiency | Worst (~1,800 wasted) | Neutral | Best |
| Consistency across models | Worst (dual-path variance) | Neutral | Best |

The LLM Expert's concern is about how the system prompt's persona framing unconsciously steers the LLM's judgment. Let me break it down concretely:

### The Two Personas

**Session Insights:** *"You are a senior staff engineer writing entries for a team's engineering knowledge base."*

- When it sees a friction point, it genuinely asks: "whose fault was this?"

- When it sees a friction point, its instinct is: "how could the user have prevented this?"

### How This Plays Out on the Same Friction Moment

Imagine this scenario: The AI used the wrong API method despite the user giving clear instructions.

| Attribution Step | Engineer Persona | Coach Persona |
|---|---|---|
| External cause? | No | No |
| Could user have prevented? | "User was clear. AI misread the docs." → **No** | "User could have pasted the exact API signature..." → **Yes** |
| Result | `ai-capability` | `user-actionable` |

Both readings are *defensible*. But the coach persona has a structural incentive to find a user-actionable angle — because that's literally its job (help the user improve). The engineer persona doesn't have that bias.

### Another Example

The AI forgot a constraint established 20 messages ago (`context-loss` friction):

| | Engineer Persona | Coach Persona |
|---|---|---|
| Assessment | "AI lost context mid-session" → `ai-capability` | "User should have restated the constraint" → `user-actionable` |

This matters because:
- **Progress tracking** filters on `attribution = 'user-actionable'` to measure user improvement
- If PQ systematically over-attributes to `user-actionable`, users appear to have **more** user-caused friction than they actually do
- Weekly Reflect narratives would say "you keep causing friction by not providing enough context" when the real issue was AI capability gaps

The Expert estimates **15-25% of attribution assignments** would shift toward `user-actionable` under the coaching persona.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
**This is a well-known phenomenon in LLM prompt engineering called "persona priming."** The system prompt's role description acts as a prior that colors all subsequent reasoning. A "security auditor" persona finds more vulnerabilities than a "developer" persona reviewing the same code. A "defense attorney" persona finds more exculpatory evidence than a "prosecutor" reviewing the same case. The effect is strongest on judgment calls at ambiguous boundaries — which is exactly where friction attribution lives.

The existing tiebreaker in the prompt — *"lean user-actionable when mixed"* (line 107) — was calibrated for the engineer persona. Stacking a coaching persona on top of that tiebreaker doubles the thumb on the scale.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

# 3-Prompt Architecture: Full Evaluation

## Q1: Quality -- Would dedicated prompts produce better output?

**Short answer: Yes, meaningfully so. This is the strongest argument for the split.**

Here's the structural problem with the current `SESSION_ANALYSIS_SYSTEM_PROMPT`. It's a 107-line system prompt that asks the model to simultaneously:

1. Be a "senior staff engineer writing entries for a team's engineering knowledge base" (knowledge capture persona)
2. Extract behavioral facets as a neutral session observer (diagnostic persona)
3. Follow two separate classification guidance blocks totaling ~1,800 tokens (FRICTION_CLASSIFICATION_GUIDANCE + EFFECTIVE_PATTERN_CLASSIFICATION_GUIDANCE)
4. Produce a JSON response with 7 top-level fields spanning two fundamentally different concerns

The persona tension is real. "Senior staff engineer" biases toward narrative and judgment. Neutral facet extraction needs the opposite -- dispassionate classification. When a model tries to satisfy both, the facet output inherits the knowledge-capture framing. Specifically:

- **Friction attribution skews "ai-capability"** because the staff engineer persona empathizes with the developer and tends to blame the tool. A neutral observer persona would more often reach "user-actionable" through the decision tree.
- **Pattern descriptions get narrative-heavy** instead of staying crisp. The knowledge-base framing encourages storytelling; diagnostic extraction needs telegraphic precision.
- **The `_reasoning` CoT fields compete for attention budget** with the insight evidence fields. Both ask for multi-sentence reasoning. The model has to allocate output tokens across 7+ distinct reasoning tasks in one generation pass.

The PQ prompt is already separate, so that's not a new split -- but it would benefit from receiving the session summary (from call 1) as pre-digested context rather than re-deriving it from raw transcript.

**Estimated quality improvement:** Moderate-to-significant for facets (10-20% better attribution accuracy, based on the known `collaborative` over-selection and `ai-capability` bias patterns). Marginal for PQ (already separate). Meaningful for insights (shorter, more focused prompt = tighter output).

## Q2: Cost -- Actual Token Impact

Let me estimate the current prompts. Token counts use the ~4 chars/token approximation for English text with JSON structures.

### Current System Prompts (Fixed Costs)

| Prompt | Characters | Est. Tokens |
|--------|-----------|-------------|
| `SESSION_ANALYSIS_SYSTEM_PROMPT` (lines 259-367) | ~5,800 chars | ~1,450 |
| Inline guidance (FRICTION + PATTERN) | ~3,600 chars | ~900 |
| **Total session analysis system** | **~9,400** | **~2,350** |
| `PROMPT_QUALITY_SYSTEM_PROMPT` (lines 694-728) | ~1,700 chars | ~425 |
| Inline PQ guidance | ~3,200 chars | ~800 |
| **Total PQ system** | **~4,900** | **~1,225** |
| `FACET_ONLY_SYSTEM_PROMPT` (lines 625-641) | ~900 chars | ~225 |
| Inline guidance (FRICTION + PATTERN, same blocks) | ~3,600 chars | ~900 |
| **Total facet-only system** | **~4,500** | **~1,125** |

### Current User Prompts (Variable Costs)

| Prompt | Fixed Template | Variable (Conversation) |
|--------|---------------|------------------------|
| `generateSessionAnalysisPrompt` | ~550 tokens | Full transcript |
| `generatePromptQualityPrompt` | ~350 tokens | Full transcript |
| `generateFacetOnlyPrompt` | ~200 tokens | First/last 20 msgs |

### Typical Session Token Profile

For a median session (~40 messages, ~15k input tokens of formatted conversation):

**Current 2-call architecture:**

| Call | System | User Template | Conversation | Output | Total |
|------|--------|--------------|--------------|--------|-------|
| Session Analysis | 2,350 | 550 | 15,000 | ~1,800 | ~19,700 |
| Prompt Quality | 1,225 | 350 | 15,000 | ~1,200 | ~17,775 |
| **Total** | | | | | **~37,475** |

Conversation tokens sent: **30,000** (15k x 2 calls)

**Proposed 3-call architecture:**

| Call | System | User Template | Conversation | Output | Total |
|------|--------|--------------|--------------|--------|-------|
| Session Insights | ~1,200 | ~400 | 15,000 | ~1,400 | ~18,000 |
| Session Facets | ~1,125 | ~200 | ~3,000* | ~600 | ~4,925 |
| Prompt Quality | ~1,225 | ~350 | 15,000 | ~1,200 | ~17,775 |
| **Total** | | | | | **~40,700** |

*Facets use first/last 20 messages (~3k tokens), same as current backfill prompt.

Conversation tokens sent: **33,000** (15k + 3k + 15k)

**Delta: +3,225 tokens (+8.6%)**

That's the key number. Not 50% more (which is what "3 calls instead of 2" intuitively suggests). The facet call is lightweight because it already uses the summary + first/last 20 message pattern from `FACET_ONLY_SYSTEM_PROMPT`.

### Cost in Dollars

Using Sonnet 3.5 pricing ($3/1M input, $15/1M output):

| Architecture | Input Cost | Output Cost | Total per Session |
|-------------|-----------|-------------|-------------------|
| Current (2 calls) | $0.0010 | $0.0005 | **$0.0015** |
| Proposed (3 calls) | $0.0011 | $0.0005 | **$0.0016** |
| Delta | | | **+$0.0001 (+6.7%)** |

Using GPT-4o ($2.50/1M input, $10/1M output): even cheaper, same ratio.

Using Haiku ($0.25/1M input, $1.25/1M output): total goes from $0.00013 to $0.00014.

**The cost argument against this architecture is negligible.** A user analyzing 100 sessions pays ~1 cent more total.

## Q3: Can the Facet Prompt Be Lightweight?

**Yes, and the infrastructure already exists.**

`FACET_ONLY_SYSTEM_PROMPT` + `generateFacetOnlyPrompt()` is already a dedicated, lightweight facet extraction prompt. It:
- Takes summary + first/last 20 messages (not full transcript)
- Includes both classification guidance blocks (FRICTION + PATTERN)
- Outputs only facet JSON (~350-600 tokens)
- Already has the `_reasoning` CoT fields, attribution decision tree, driver classification

The proposed "Session Facets" call would essentially BE this prompt, possibly with minor enhancements:
- Could receive the session summary from the Insights call (if sequenced) for better context
- Could receive the full transcript for sessions under ~5k tokens (short sessions where first/last 20 = all messages anyway)

The existing facet-only prompt is battle-tested through backfill. Promoting it to first-class status is a lateral move, not a new build.

## Q4: Sequencing and Parallelism

**Optimal execution plan:**

```
                     ┌─── Session Insights (full transcript) ──┐
    [Format Messages] │                                         ├─ [Save All]
                     └─── Session Facets (summary + sample) ───┘
                                       │
                              (optional: feed summary to PQ)
                                       │
                              ─── Prompt Quality (full transcript) ───
```

**Insights and Facets: run in parallel.** They have zero data dependency. Both read from the same message set but extract orthogonal information. This is the best possible outcome for latency -- the 3-call architecture can complete in the same wall-clock time as the current 2-call architecture if Insights and Facets run concurrently.

**PQ: can run in parallel too, or sequenced.** PQ doesn't need output from the other two. It could benefit slightly from receiving the session summary (from Insights) to anchor its assessment, but the current PQ prompt doesn't use a summary at all -- it derives its own understanding from the transcript. So all three could run in parallel.

**Recommendation:** Run all 3 in parallel. No sequencing needed. This means the 3-call architecture adds zero latency over the current 2-call architecture (and the facet call finishes first since it's lightest).

```typescript
// Conceptual execution
const [insightsResult, facetsResult, pqResult] = await Promise.all([
  analyzeSessionInsights(session, messages, options),
  extractSessionFacets(session, messages, options),    // lightweight
  analyzePromptQuality(session, messages, options),
]);
```

## Q5: Is This Actually Practical?

**Yes.** Here's why:

**Cost:** +$0.0001/session (~7% increase). Irrelevant at any scale a local-first tool will see.

**Latency:** Zero increase if parallelized. The facet call (~5k input) finishes in ~1-2s while the other two take ~5-8s each.

**Complexity:** Lower, not higher. Currently `analyzeSession()` is a 180-line function that handles insights + facets + session character + chunking + facet fallback for chunked sessions. Splitting means:
- `analyzeSessionInsights()`: insights + summary + session character only. No facet schema, no facet parsing, no facet fallback logic. ~100 lines.
- `extractSessionFacets()`: facets only. Already exists as `extractFacetsOnly()`. ~55 lines.
- `analyzePromptQuality()`: unchanged. ~90 lines.

The chunking complexity in `analyzeSession()` (lines 129-211) exists partly because facets "can't be merged across chunks" -- so there's a special fallback that runs the facet-only prompt after chunk merging. In the 3-call architecture, this goes away entirely. Facets always use the lightweight prompt. The insights prompt handles chunking for insights only, which CAN be merged.

**UX:** No "three-button problem." The dashboard trigger stays as one "Analyze" action that fires all three calls internally. The user sees one progress bar. The three calls are an implementation detail.

## Q6: The Backfill Angle

This is where the architecture becomes genuinely elegant.

**Currently:** There are two distinct code paths for facets:
1. Primary: facets extracted as part of `analyzeSession()` (embedded in session insights prompt)
2. Backfill: `extractFacetsOnly()` uses `FACET_ONLY_SYSTEM_PROMPT` (standalone lightweight prompt)

These produce subtly different output because they use different prompts with different personas. The session analysis prompt asks a "senior staff engineer" to also classify facets; the backfill prompt asks a neutral assessor. This means backfilled facets and primary-path facets have different quality characteristics -- a consistency problem that's hard to detect.

**With 3-call architecture:** There is ONE facet prompt, ONE code path. Primary analysis and backfill use the exact same function: `extractSessionFacets()`. Backfill literally becomes "re-run the facet call." No special backfill prompt, no divergent behavior, no consistency risk.

Similarly, the "outdated detection" UNION query (which currently checks for missing `_reasoning`, missing `driver`, missing `category` across multiple arms) becomes simpler: outdated = facets were generated by an older version of the single facet prompt.

## Summary Assessment

| Dimension | Current (2-call) | Proposed (3-call) |
|-----------|-----------------|-------------------|
| Cost per session | ~$0.0015 | ~$0.0016 (+7%) |
| Latency (parallel) | ~8s | ~8s (same) |
| Facet quality | Mixed persona, knowledge-capture bias | Dedicated neutral observer |
| Friction attribution accuracy | Moderate (staff-eng bias toward ai-capability) | Better (neutral persona + focused attention) |
| Code complexity | High (facets embedded in insights, chunking fallback, two facet paths) | Lower (3 focused functions, one facet path) |
| Backfill consistency | Two different prompts produce different quality | One prompt, one path |
| Prompt maintainability | 107-line system prompt doing 2 jobs | 3 focused prompts, each ~30-50 lines |

**The honest assessment:** I was wrong to dismiss this as "the right answer if cost didn't matter." Cost doesn't matter here -- the delta is $0.0001/session. The real wins are:

1. **Better facet quality** from a dedicated, neutral persona (the strongest argument)
2. **Simpler code** by eliminating the dual facet path and chunking fallback
3. **Backfill consistency** from a single facet extraction path
4. **Zero latency cost** when parallelized
5. **Easier prompt iteration** -- each prompt is half the size and serves one purpose

The only genuine risk is the additional API call adding a failure point. But all three calls are already independently retryable, and the facet call is the lightest (fastest to complete, cheapest to retry).

**Verdict: This is the right architecture, and cost is not a meaningful counterargument.**
